

AGENTIC AI · HANDS-ON LAB PROGRAM
The 14-Lab Worklet Build Book
How the 14 hands-on labs evolve each of the 15 worklets into a full, team-owned agentic system


15 self-contained chapters · node graph · agents & interconnections · data model · solution pseudocode · lab-by-lab plan
Version 1.0 · August 2026 · Design principle: agents draft and flag; a named human approves.

Worklets in This Book
Each chapter is self-contained: it restates the 14-lab method, then applies it to one worklet — node graph, agents and their interconnections, data model, solution pseudocode, and the full 14-lab evolution.

CHAPTER 1
Programming Lab Examination & Auto-Evaluation Agent
Faculty-chosen questions and marks; test-case-based auto-grading of student code — marks capped at the faculty maximum, with where-you-went-wrong feedback.
Human authority: faculty examiner.   Primary output: graded lab submission.
The 14-Lab Method (Recap)
Every worklet in this book is built the same way: as a coordinator-plus-workers agentic system that is grown, one capability at a time, through 14 hands-on labs. This chapter is self-contained — it restates the method, the node-graph legend and the pseudocode conventions before applying them to this worklet — so it can be handed to a team on its own.
The guiding design principle is constant across all worklets: agents draft and flag; a named human approves. No worklet takes a consequential action autonomously — the human authority named in this chapter signs off before anything is published or sent.
The four phases
Reading the node graph
Navy — the apniLeap AURA Coordinator: owns the run-state, plan, budgets and audit log.
Light blue — specialist agents, each with one responsibility and a typed message contract.
Amber — the human review gate: a hard stop; the named human is the final authority.
Green — governed publish/act: versioned, auditable, reversible.
Dashed grey — coordinator oversight of every agent; a red back-edge is validation → regenerate.
Pseudocode conventions
Pseudocode is language-neutral: Coordinator orchestrates; Agent.step(ctx) is one specialist doing its job on the shared run-context; a human gate returns approve / edit / override.
The blueprint/plan is locked before work begins; validation is a hard gate; the human approves before publish.
1.1  Worklet Overview
Lab examinations in computer-science courses — Operating Systems, DBMS, Data Structures, Algorithms, Machine Learning, Deep Learning, and any major programming-heavy course — require faculty to set problems grounded in what was actually taught and practiced, then grade many student submissions by running them. Doing this by hand is slow, inconsistent across evaluators, and rarely leaves students with specific feedback on what failed.
This worklet proposes a Programming Lab Examination & Auto-Evaluation Agent that drafts questions from the course syllabus and completed lab activities for faculty to choose from (with faculty setting the marks per question), accepts each student's soft-copy code, runs it against faculty-provided test cases in an isolated sandbox, awards marks capped at the faculty-assigned maximum, and drafts a where-you-went-wrong report — with faculty approving the questions, marks, and feedback before anything is published.
Stakeholders: Faculty, Students, HoD, COE (Exam Cell)
Problems heard
Setting lab questions that match the covered syllabus and completed activities is done manually each exam.
Running and grading a large batch of student code against test cases by hand is slow and error-prone.
Marks vary by evaluator and can exceed or misalign with the intended maximum for a question.
Students get a score but no clear explanation of which test cases failed or why.
Test cases and marking are not consistently tied to the specific problem statement.
1.2  Target Final Solution (Definition of Done)
The capstone (Lab 14) is complete when, for a real case, the worklet turns a request into a validated, evidence-grounded graded lab submission that passes every check, is reviewed and approved by faculty examiner, and is published with a full audit trail — with light human edits, not a rewrite.
Capabilities
Success metrics (KPIs)
100% of validation checks pass before faculty examiner review (e.g. awarded marks never exceed the faculty maximum per question).
Every graded lab submission is grounded in the faculty-provided test cases and rubric, with an evidence record.
Zero consequential actions without faculty examiner's approval; a complete audit trail and one-click rollback.
1.3  System at a Glance — the Node Graph
A coordinator-plus-workers system: the apniLeap AURA Coordinator holds the plan, run-state and audit log and drives the pipeline; validation is a hard gate; the human review is a hard stop before publish.

Figure 1 — the Programming Lab Examination & Auto-Evaluation Agent agent graph. Amber = human gate; green = governed publish; dashed = coordinator oversight.
End-to-end flow
Faculty loads the course syllabus and the list of lab activities completed before the exam.
The Question Generation Agent drafts candidate questions grounded in the covered syllabus and completed activities; faculty selects the questions and sets the maximum marks per question.
The Test-Case Intake Agent ingests the faculty-provided test cases (visible + hidden) with expected outputs and per-case weights, mapped to each problem.
The Submission Intake Agent accepts each student's soft-copy code, detects the language, and timestamps it against the exam window.
The Sandbox Execution Agent compiles and runs each submission against every test case in an isolated, resource-limited sandbox and records pass/fail, runtime, and errors.
The Code Similarity Agent flags submissions that are suspiciously similar to one another for faculty review.
The Scoring Agent computes marks from the weights of passed test cases, capped at the faculty-assigned maximum for that question.
The Feedback Agent drafts a where-you-went-wrong report — failing cases, expected versus actual output, errors, and likely cause.
Faculty reviews flagged, borderline, and high-similarity submissions, approves or adjusts marks and feedback, and the Result Publishing & Archive Agent releases results and archives the record.

1.10  The 14-Lab Evolution
Each lab is a working increment that adds one capability to this worklet and activates specific nodes. Ship the deliverable and pass acceptance before advancing — by Lab 14 they compose into the deployed graded lab submission system.

Lab 1 — Agent vs Chatbot — the graded lab submission planner loop
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Establish the agent loop (clarify → plan → revise) instead of a one-shot chatbot answer.
What it adds to this worklet.  A minimal planner that clarifies the request, proposes a plan for producing the graded lab submission, and revises on feedback — the seed this worklet grows from.
Nodes activated.  Coordinator loop (no specialist nodes yet).
Pitfall to avoid.  Don't answer in one shot — without clarify/revise it's a chatbot, not an agent.
Lab 1 — plan & revise

Lab 2 — Tool-Using Agent — call data & compute
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Give the agent tools it must call to act, rather than answering from memory.
What it adds to this worklet.  Readers for the question & test-case bank and student code submissions, plus a domain calculator/checker, so the planner acts on real data.
Nodes activated.  Tool layer feeding the early data agents.
Pitfall to avoid.  Never assert a fact in the prompt that a tool should verify.
Lab 2 — typed tools

Lab 3 — Skill Creation — reusable plan & format skills
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Package repeatable capability as reusable, schema-typed skills.
What it adds to this worklet.  A plan/blueprint skill and a formatting skill that renders the graded lab submission — both reusable across cases.
Nodes activated.  Plan node + Assembly node (as skills).
Pitfall to avoid.  Keep skills case-agnostic and typed; hard-coding one case breaks reuse.
Lab 3 — skill interface

Lab 4 — Memory & Retrieval — state vs stored
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Separate short-term run state from long-term stored knowledge.
What it adds to this worklet.  Session state (the current draft) vs stored reference — the question & test-case bank and the history of past student submissions — retrieved by tags and by similarity.
Nodes activated.  Retrieval node; seeds validation/no-repeat.
Pitfall to avoid.  Don't conflate run state with the archive — mixing them corrupts the baseline.
Lab 4 — memory split

Lab 5 — MCP-Style Connector — one server for all sources
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Expose every data source through one typed connector.
What it adds to this worklet.  A single connector fronting the question & test-case bank, student code submissions and the rubric & marks scheme so every agent reaches data the same governed way.
Nodes activated.  Connector under the data agents.
Pitfall to avoid.  No agent should touch a source directly; a bypass defeats governance later.
Lab 5 — connector

Lab 6 — Runtime — skills, tools, memory, audit
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Run the agent inside a controlled runtime with a full audit log.
What it adds to this worklet.  A runtime binding skills + tools + memory behind the loop, enforcing budgets and approval gates and logging every step — the backbone later labs plug into.
Nodes activated.  Coordinator runtime around all nodes.
Pitfall to avoid.  Log inputs and outputs, not just messages; checkpoint before risky steps or resume loses state.
Lab 6 — controlled loop

Lab 7 — Agentic Node Graph — the full pipeline
Phase: B · Orchestration     Goal: Wire the specialist agents into one explicit graph with state and branching.
What it adds to this worklet.  The complete pipeline as a node graph: Question Generation Agent, Test-Case Intake Agent, Submission Intake Agent → … → faculty examiner gate → publish, with a validation → regenerate back-edge.
Nodes activated.  All core nodes + the back-edge.
Pitfall to avoid.  Bound the retry edge — an unbounded regenerate loop can spin forever.
Lab 7 — graph

Lab 8 — Parallel Swarm — many student submissions, then merge
Phase: B · Orchestration     Goal: Parallelise the per-unit work and select the best.
What it adds to this worklet.  Several worker agents process student submissions in parallel; a merge/selector picks the best per the plan — cutting latency and improving quality.
Nodes activated.  Parallel worker fan-out + merge.
Pitfall to avoid.  Merge strictly by plan alignment; picking the 'nicest' output quietly breaks coverage.
Lab 8 — fan-out/in

Lab 9 — Deep Research — ground & evidence
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Ground every output in cited, in-scope evidence.
What it adds to this worklet.  For each student submission the agent builds an evidence record grounded in the faculty-provided test cases and rubric, so validity is auditable and out-of-scope content can't slip through.
Nodes activated.  Grounding step into the validation check.
Pitfall to avoid.  Require a real source; a plausible-sounding but ungrounded item must be rejected, not kept.
Lab 9 — grounding

Lab 10 — Ops Resilience — validate, diagnose, fix
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Make the pipeline self-healing when validation fails.
What it adds to this worklet.  When a check fails (awarded marks never exceed the faculty maximum per question; every test case runs inside the sandbox), the agent diagnoses the exact failing constraint and regenerates only the offending part.
Nodes activated.  Validation node + self-healing loop.
Pitfall to avoid.  Regenerate only the failing part — rebuilding everything wastes budget and churns good work.
Lab 10 — diagnose & fix

Lab 11 — SDLC Factory — spec → tested output
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Treat the plan as a spec and the output as a tested implementation.
What it adds to this worklet.  The worklet becomes a factory: plan in, a graded lab submission out that provably passes an automated acceptance suite before faculty examiner sees it.
Nodes activated.  Validation gates the whole graph.
Pitfall to avoid.  The acceptance suite is the gate; never let a result skip to the human without a green report.
Lab 11 — spec→artifact

Lab 12 — Safety & Governance — approval, eval, rollback
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Add confidentiality, approval, evaluation and rollback.
What it adds to this worklet.  Access control and confidentiality, the faculty examiner approval gate as a hard stop, evaluation metrics, versioning and one-click rollback.
Nodes activated.  Human gate + governance node.
Pitfall to avoid.  Confidentiality and rollback are requirements, not extras.
Lab 12 — governed release

Lab 13 — Discipline-Specific Agents — domain specialisation
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Specialise the worklet to its domain variants.
What it adds to this worklet.  Pluggable specialists — language/paradigm packs (C/Java/Python, SQL/DBMS, OS system-calls, DS/algorithms) — so outputs match the specific style and constraints of each case.
Nodes activated.  Specialist into the generation node.
Pitfall to avoid.  Every specialist output needs a checkable scheme; clever but vague outputs are unusable.
Lab 13 — specialist plug-in

Lab 14 — Capstone — team-owned system
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Integrate everything into a deployed, team-owned system.
What it adds to this worklet.  The full worklet: connector + runtime + node graph + parallel work + grounding + self-healing + governance + specialists, with a faculty examiner dashboard — delivered and demoed end to end.
Nodes activated.  Every node, deployed.
Pitfall to avoid.  Integrate, don't rewrite — if the labs kept the contracts, the capstone is wiring.
Lab 14 — end-to-end

1.11  Reference Architecture, Pilot & MVP
Reference architecture
Data capture: course syllabus, completed lab activities, faculty questions and maximum marks, faculty test-case suites, and student soft-copy submissions.
Data layer: per-question schema for problem, max marks, and weighted test cases; per-submission schema for code, execution results, marks, and feedback.
AI layer: syllabus-grounded question drafting, language detection, root-cause/where-you-went-wrong feedback, and code-similarity detection.
Execution layer: an isolated, network-disabled, resource- and time-limited sandbox runner that compiles/runs code and records per-test-case pass/fail.
Workflow layer: faculty selection of questions/marks/test cases, review queue, capped scoring, publishing, and dashboard.
Governance layer: marks-cap enforcement, hidden-test-case confidentiality, sandbox isolation, per-student privacy, and an audit log of marks and approvals.
Pilot artifacts
One lab exam configured for a course (e.g., Data Structures or DBMS) with faculty-selected questions and per-question maximum marks.
A faculty test-case suite (visible + hidden, weighted) mapped to each question.
A sandboxed execution and capped-scoring run over a batch of student soft copies, with a similarity check.
A where-you-went-wrong feedback report and a faculty approval queue before publishing.
Suggested MVP roadmap
Risks & mitigations
1.12  Guardrails, Metrics, Milestones & Glossary
Guardrails
Never award marks above the faculty-assigned maximum for a question — marks are always less than or equal to the maximum.
Grade only against the faculty-provided test cases; never invent, modify, or drop a test case.
Never generate a question outside the covered syllabus and completed activities; faculty choose the questions and the marks.
Always run student code in a network-disabled, resource- and time-limited sandbox; never expose one student's code to another.
Flag high-similarity submissions for review only; never auto-penalize.
Never publish marks or feedback without faculty approval; keep hidden test cases confidential and provide only evidence-based feedback.
Delivery milestones

Glossary

CHAPTER 2
Course Project, Notes & Assignment Organizer
One coordinator for notes sharing, assignment distribution, and project tracking.
Human authority: course faculty.   Primary output: organised course workspace (notes, assignments, grades).
The 14-Lab Method (Recap)
Every worklet in this book is built the same way: as a coordinator-plus-workers agentic system that is grown, one capability at a time, through 14 hands-on labs. This chapter is self-contained — it restates the method, the node-graph legend and the pseudocode conventions before applying them to this worklet — so it can be handed to a team on its own.
The guiding design principle is constant across all worklets: agents draft and flag; a named human approves. No worklet takes a consequential action autonomously — the human authority named in this chapter signs off before anything is published or sent.
The four phases
Reading the node graph
Navy — the apniLeap AURA Coordinator: owns the run-state, plan, budgets and audit log.
Light blue — specialist agents, each with one responsibility and a typed message contract.
Amber — the human review gate: a hard stop; the named human is the final authority.
Green — governed publish/act: versioned, auditable, reversible.
Dashed grey — coordinator oversight of every agent; a red back-edge is validation → regenerate.
Pseudocode conventions
Pseudocode is language-neutral: Coordinator orchestrates; Agent.step(ctx) is one specialist doing its job on the shared run-context; a human gate returns approve / edit / override.
The blueprint/plan is locked before work begins; validation is a hard gate; the human approves before publish.
2.1  Worklet Overview
A single course generates three ongoing streams of work: sharing notes/materials, distributing and collecting assignments, and tracking course projects through milestones. Each is currently managed through separate folders, spreadsheets, and manual reminders.
This worklet proposes a Course Project, Notes & Assignment Organizer that manages all three streams under one coordinator: a versioned notes repository, assignment distribution and collection with plagiarism checks and grading assistance, and project milestone tracking — with faculty approving all grades and feedback before publishing.
Stakeholders: Faculty, Students, HoD
Problems heard
Notes and materials get shared through different channels each week, so students aren't sure where the current version is.
Assignment distribution, deadline reminders, and submission collection are manually tracked per student.
Grading takes significant time, and consistent feedback across a large batch is hard to maintain.
Similarity/plagiarism checking across submissions is inconsistent or skipped under time pressure.
Course projects have milestones, but there's no systematic tracking of which group is on schedule.
2.2  Target Final Solution (Definition of Done)
The capstone (Lab 14) is complete when, for a real case, the worklet turns a request into a validated, evidence-grounded organised course workspace (notes, assignments, grades) that passes every check, is reviewed and approved by course faculty, and is published with a full audit trail — with light human edits, not a rewrite.
Capabilities
Success metrics (KPIs)
100% of validation checks pass before course faculty review (e.g. a draft grade never exceeds the rubric maximum).
Every organised course workspace (notes, assignments, grades) is grounded in the faculty-approved rubric and syllabus, with an evidence record.
Zero consequential actions without course faculty's approval; a complete audit trail and one-click rollback.
2.3  System at a Glance — the Node Graph
A coordinator-plus-workers system: the apniLeap AURA Coordinator holds the plan, run-state and audit log and drives the pipeline; validation is a hard gate; the human review is a hard stop before publish.

Figure 2 — the Course Project, Notes & Assignment Organizer agent graph. Amber = human gate; green = governed publish; dashed = coordinator oversight.
End-to-end flow
The Notes Repository Agent keeps one current, versioned set of materials organized by unit/topic.
The Assignment Distribution Agent releases assignments on schedule and sends deadline reminders.
The Submission Collection Agent gathers student submissions and timestamps them against the deadline.
The Plagiarism/Similarity Agent checks each submission and flags high-similarity cases for review.
The Grading Assistance Agent drafts a grade and feedback against the rubric for faculty to review.
The Project Milestone Tracking Agent checks each group's progress against defined checkpoints.
Faculty reviews drafted grades, feedback, and similarity flags, and approves or edits them.
The Grade Publishing & Archive Agent publishes approved grades/feedback and archives the submission record.

2.11  Reference Architecture, Pilot & MVP
Reference architecture
Data capture: notes uploads, assignment briefs/rubrics, student submissions, project milestone reports.
Data layer: canonical schema for materials, assignments, submissions, grades, and milestones.
AI layer: similarity checking, rubric-aligned grading drafts, milestone status summarization.
Workflow layer: reminders, faculty review queue, grade publishing, dashboard.
Governance layer: access control per role, version history, audit log of grades published.
Pilot artifacts
A versioned notes repository for one course unit.
One assignment cycle: distribution, collection, similarity check, draft grading, and faculty-approved publishing.
A milestone tracker for one course project with at least two checkpoints.
A submission dashboard showing on-time/late status and similarity flags.
Suggested MVP roadmap
Risks & mitigations
2.12  Guardrails, Metrics, Milestones & Glossary
Guardrails
Never publish a grade or feedback without faculty approval.
Never make a final plagiarism/similarity determination automatically — always flag for human review.
Never alter a submission timestamp.
Keep clear version history for notes and materials.
Keep a full audit log of every grade published and who approved it.
Delivery milestones

Glossary

CHAPTER 3
Email Organizer Agent
Triage, draft, and track faculty email — with faculty approving every reply that goes out.
Human authority: the mailbox owner.   Primary output: triaged inbox with draft replies.
The 14-Lab Method (Recap)
Every worklet in this book is built the same way: as a coordinator-plus-workers agentic system that is grown, one capability at a time, through 14 hands-on labs. This chapter is self-contained — it restates the method, the node-graph legend and the pseudocode conventions before applying them to this worklet — so it can be handed to a team on its own.

Dashed grey — coordinator oversight of every agent; a red back-edge is validation → regenerate.
Pseudocode conventions
Pseudocode is language-neutral: Coordinator orchestrates; Agent.step(ctx) is one specialist doing its job on the shared run-context; a human gate returns approve / edit / override.
The blueprint/plan is locked before work begins; validation is a hard gate; the human approves before publish.
3.1  Worklet Overview
Faculty inboxes mix urgent administrative requests, student queries, meeting requests, and low-priority notices. Sorting, prioritizing, and replying consistently is time-consuming and easy to fall behind on.
This worklet proposes an Email Organizer Agent that classifies incoming mail, drafts replies for review, extracts action items and meeting requests, and tracks follow-ups — without ever sending anything without faculty approval.
Stakeholders: Faculty
Problems heard
Important requests (admin deadlines, student issues) can be buried under routine notices.
Similar questions get answered from scratch each time instead of reusing a standard response.
Meeting requests embedded in email threads are easy to miss or double-book against.
Action items mentioned in passing within longer emails are not tracked anywhere.
There is no consistent follow-up reminder for emails that were meant to be answered later.
3.2  Target Final Solution (Definition of Done)
The capstone (Lab 14) is complete when, for a real case, the worklet turns a request into a validated, evidence-grounded triaged inbox with draft replies that passes every check, is reviewed and approved by the mailbox owner, and is published with a full audit trail — with light human edits, not a rewrite.
Capabilities
Success metrics (KPIs)
100% of validation checks pass before the mailbox owner review (e.g. no email is sent without the owner's approval).
Every triaged inbox with draft replies is grounded in the actual thread history and the owner's past replies, with an evidence record.
Zero consequential actions without the mailbox owner's approval; a complete audit trail and one-click rollback.
3.3  System at a Glance — the Node Graph
A coordinator-plus-workers system: the apniLeap AURA Coordinator holds the plan, run-state and audit log and drives the pipeline; validation is a hard gate; the human review is a hard stop before publish.

Figure 3 — the Email Organizer Agent agent graph. Amber = human gate; green = governed publish; dashed = coordinator oversight.
End-to-end flow
The Ingestion & Classification Agent tags each incoming email by sender category and content type.
The Priority & Urgency Agent ranks emails by deadline signals and escalation language.
The Draft Reply Agent proposes a reply, using the standard-response library where a close match exists.
The Action-Item Extraction Agent pulls out tasks, deadlines, and commitments into a tracked list.
The Follow-up/Reminder Agent flags emails marked “reply later” and resurfaces them on schedule.
The Calendar & Task Sync Agent matches meeting requests to faculty's calendar and proposes slots.
Faculty reviews the draft queue, approves, edits, or discards each draft.
The Archive & Logging Agent sends approved replies, logs the exchange, and archives resolved items.

How the coordinator runs each stage — what is human-gated, what runs in parallel, what is sequential.

3.10  The 14-Lab Evolution
Each lab is a working increment that adds one capability to this worklet and activates specific nodes. Ship the deliverable and pass acceptance before advancing — by Lab 14 they compose into the deployed triaged inbox with draft replies system.

Lab 1 — Agent vs Chatbot — the triaged inbox with draft replies planner loop
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Establish the agent loop (clarify → plan → revise) instead of a one-shot chatbot answer.
What it adds to this worklet.  A minimal planner that clarifies the request, proposes a plan for producing the triaged inbox with draft replies, and revises on feedback — the seed this worklet grows from.
Nodes activated.  Coordinator loop (no specialist nodes yet).
Pitfall to avoid.  Don't answer in one shot — without clarify/revise it's a chatbot, not an agent.
Lab 1 — plan & revise

Lab 2 — Tool-Using Agent — call data & compute
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Give the agent tools it must call to act, rather than answering from memory.
What it adds to this worklet.  Readers for the inbox & threads and contacts & prior replies, plus a domain calculator/checker, so the planner acts on real data.
Nodes activated.  Tool layer feeding the early data agents.
Pitfall to avoid.  Never assert a fact in the prompt that a tool should verify.
Lab 2 — typed tools

Lab 3 — Skill Creation — reusable plan & format skills
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Package repeatable capability as reusable, schema-typed skills.
What it adds to this worklet.  A plan/blueprint skill and a formatting skill that renders the triaged inbox with draft replies — both reusable across cases.
Nodes activated.  Plan node + Assembly node (as skills).
Pitfall to avoid.  Keep skills case-agnostic and typed; hard-coding one case breaks reuse.
Lab 3 — skill interface

Lab 4 — Memory & Retrieval — state vs stored
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Separate short-term run state from long-term stored knowledge.
What it adds to this worklet.  Session state (the current draft) vs stored reference — the inbox & threads and the history of past email threads — retrieved by tags and by similarity.
Nodes activated.  Retrieval node; seeds validation/no-repeat.
Pitfall to avoid.  Don't conflate run state with the archive — mixing them corrupts the baseline.
Lab 4 — memory split

Lab 5 — MCP-Style Connector — one server for all sources
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Expose every data source through one typed connector.
What it adds to this worklet.  A single connector fronting the inbox & threads, contacts & prior replies and the calendar & task list so every agent reaches data the same governed way.
Nodes activated.  Connector under the data agents.
Pitfall to avoid.  No agent should touch a source directly; a bypass defeats governance later.
Lab 5 — connector

Lab 6 — Runtime — skills, tools, memory, audit
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Run the agent inside a controlled runtime with a full audit log.
What it adds to this worklet.  A runtime binding skills + tools + memory behind the loop, enforcing budgets and approval gates and logging every step — the backbone later labs plug into.
Nodes activated.  Coordinator runtime around all nodes.
Pitfall to avoid.  Log inputs and outputs, not just messages; checkpoint before risky steps or resume loses state.
Lab 6 — controlled loop

Lab 7 — Agentic Node Graph — the full pipeline
Phase: B · Orchestration     Goal: Wire the specialist agents into one explicit graph with state and branching.
What it adds to this worklet.  The complete pipeline as a node graph: Ingestion & Classification Agent, Priority & Urgency Agent, Draft Reply Agent → … → the mailbox owner gate → publish, with a validation → regenerate back-edge.
Nodes activated.  All core nodes + the back-edge.
Pitfall to avoid.  Bound the retry edge — an unbounded regenerate loop can spin forever.
Lab 7 — graph

Lab 8 — Parallel Swarm — many email threads, then merge
Phase: B · Orchestration     Goal: Parallelise the per-unit work and select the best.
What it adds to this worklet.  Several worker agents process email threads in parallel; a merge/selector picks the best per the plan — cutting latency and improving quality.
Nodes activated.  Parallel worker fan-out + merge.
Pitfall to avoid.  Merge strictly by plan alignment; picking the 'nicest' output quietly breaks coverage.
Lab 8 — fan-out/in

Lab 9 — Deep Research — ground & evidence
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Ground every output in cited, in-scope evidence.
What it adds to this worklet.  For each email thread the agent builds an evidence record grounded in the actual thread history and the owner's past replies, so validity is auditable and out-of-scope content can't slip through.
Nodes activated.  Grounding step into the validation check.
Pitfall to avoid.  Require a real source; a plausible-sounding but ungrounded item must be rejected, not kept.
Lab 9 — grounding

Lab 10 — Ops Resilience — validate, diagnose, fix
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Make the pipeline self-healing when validation fails.
What it adds to this worklet.  When a check fails (no email is sent without the owner's approval; each draft reply cites its thread context), the agent diagnoses the exact failing constraint and regenerates only the offending part.
Nodes activated.  Validation node + self-healing loop.
Pitfall to avoid.  Regenerate only the failing part — rebuilding everything wastes budget and churns good work.
Lab 10 — diagnose & fix

Lab 11 — SDLC Factory — spec → tested output
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Treat the plan as a spec and the output as a tested implementation.
What it adds to this worklet.  The worklet becomes a factory: plan in, a triaged inbox with draft replies out that provably passes an automated acceptance suite before the mailbox owner sees it.
Nodes activated.  Validation gates the whole graph.
Pitfall to avoid.  The acceptance suite is the gate; never let a result skip to the human without a green report.
Lab 11 — spec→artifact

Lab 12 — Safety & Governance — approval, eval, rollback
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Add confidentiality, approval, evaluation and rollback.
What it adds to this worklet.  Access control and confidentiality, the the mailbox owner approval gate as a hard stop, evaluation metrics, versioning and one-click rollback.
Nodes activated.  Human gate + governance node.
Pitfall to avoid.  Confidentiality and rollback are requirements, not extras.
Lab 12 — governed release

Lab 13 — Discipline-Specific Agents — domain specialisation
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Specialise the worklet to its domain variants.
What it adds to this worklet.  Pluggable specialists — category handlers (scheduling, approvals, newsletters, VIP senders) — so outputs match the specific style and constraints of each case.
Nodes activated.  Specialist into the generation node.
Pitfall to avoid.  Every specialist output needs a checkable scheme; clever but vague outputs are unusable.
Lab 13 — specialist plug-in

Lab 14 — Capstone — team-owned system
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Integrate everything into a deployed, team-owned system.
What it adds to this worklet.  The full worklet: connector + runtime + node graph + parallel work + grounding + self-healing + governance + specialists, with a the mailbox owner dashboard — delivered and demoed end to end.
Nodes activated.  Every node, deployed.
Pitfall to avoid.  Integrate, don't rewrite — if the labs kept the contracts, the capstone is wiring.
Lab 14 — end-to-end

3.11  Reference Architecture, Pilot & MVP
Reference architecture
Data capture: connected inbox folders and a faculty-maintained standard-response library.
Data layer: canonical schema for sender category, urgency, action items, and meeting requests.
AI layer: classification, priority ranking, draft generation, and extraction agents.
Workflow layer: review queue, calendar sync, follow-up scheduling, dashboard.
Governance layer: access scope, audit logging of sends, no-auto-send enforcement.
Pilot artifacts
A one-week triaged inbox view with priority ranking.
A draft-reply queue using an initial standard-response library of 10–15 common questions.
An action-item and meeting-request extraction list for the same week.
A follow-up digest for emails flagged “reply later.”
Suggested MVP roadmap
Risks & mitigations
3.12  Guardrails, Metrics, Milestones & Glossary
Guardrails
Never send an email without explicit faculty approval.
Never mark a task or action item complete without confirmation.
Never confirm a meeting time without faculty approval.
Keep an audit log of every reply sent and every approval given.
Restrict inbox access strictly to the faculty's own account and role.
Delivery milestones

Glossary

CHAPTER 4
Meeting Organizer Agent
From scheduling to signed-off minutes and tracked action items.
Human authority: the meeting owner.   Primary output: scheduled meeting with agenda and minutes.
The 14-Lab Method (Recap)
Every worklet in this book is built the same way: as a coordinator-plus-workers agentic system that is grown, one capability at a time, through 14 hands-on labs. This chapter is self-contained — it restates the method, the node-graph legend and the pseudocode conventions before applying them to this worklet — so it can be handed to a team on its own.
The guiding design principle is constant across all worklets: agents draft and flag; a named human approves. No worklet takes a consequential action autonomously — the human authority named in this chapter signs off before anything is published or sent.
The four phases
Reading the node graph
Navy — the apniLeap AURA Coordinator: owns the run-state, plan, budgets and audit log.
Light blue — specialist agents, each with one responsibility and a typed message contract.
Amber — the human review gate: a hard stop; the named human is the final authority.
Green — governed publish/act: versioned, auditable, reversible.
Dashed grey — coordinator oversight of every agent; a red back-edge is validation → regenerate.
Pseudocode conventions
Pseudocode is language-neutral: Coordinator orchestrates; Agent.step(ctx) is one specialist doing its job on the shared run-context; a human gate returns approve / edit / override.
The blueprint/plan is locked before work begins; validation is a hard gate; the human approves before publish.
4.1  Worklet Overview
Organizing meetings — department, committee, or project — involves finding a common slot, preparing an agenda, capturing minutes, and tracking action items afterward. Each step is currently manual and easy to lose track of across multiple recurring meetings.
This worklet proposes a Meeting Organizer Agent that handles scheduling, agenda preparation, minutes capture, and action-item tracking, with faculty approving the final minutes and any resulting commitments.
Stakeholders: Faculty, HoD, Committee members
Problems heard
Finding a common slot across several busy calendars takes repeated back-and-forth.
Agendas are not consistently prepared in advance, so meetings run unfocused.
Minutes, when taken, vary in quality and are sometimes delayed by days.
Action items agreed in one meeting are often not tracked into the next one.
Room or resource booking (projector, video link) is handled separately and can be forgotten.
4.2  Target Final Solution (Definition of Done)
The capstone (Lab 14) is complete when, for a real case, the worklet turns a request into a validated, evidence-grounded scheduled meeting with agenda and minutes that passes every check, is reviewed and approved by the meeting owner, and is published with a full audit trail — with light human edits, not a rewrite.
Capabilities
Success metrics (KPIs)
100% of validation checks pass before the meeting owner review (e.g. no invite is sent without the owner's approval).
Every scheduled meeting with agenda and minutes is grounded in participant availability and prior meeting notes, with an evidence record.
Zero consequential actions without the meeting owner's approval; a complete audit trail and one-click rollback.
4.3  System at a Glance — the Node Graph
A coordinator-plus-workers system: the apniLeap AURA Coordinator holds the plan, run-state and audit log and drives the pipeline; validation is a hard gate; the human review is a hard stop before publish.

Figure 4 — the Meeting Organizer Agent agent graph. Amber = human gate; green = governed publish; dashed = coordinator oversight.
End-to-end flow
The Scheduling & Availability Agent proposes common slots across participant calendars.
The Agenda Preparation Agent drafts a structured agenda from the stated purpose and carried-over action items.
The Resource & Room Booking Agent reserves the room, video link, or equipment needed.
During or after the meeting, the Transcription & Minutes Agent drafts structured minutes from notes or a transcript.
The Action-Item Tracker Agent extracts new action items with owners and due dates, and carries forward open ones.
The Follow-up Reminder Agent nudges owners of open action items ahead of the next meeting.
Faculty reviews and approves the final minutes and action-item list.
The Distribution & Logging Agent circulates approved minutes and updates the dashboard.

CHAPTER 5
Curriculum Intelligence System
Continuous curriculum-vs-industry mapping, ACM/APNI benchmarking, and evidence-linked revision recommendations.
Human authority: the curriculum committee / HoD.   Primary output: curriculum revision recommendation.
The 14-Lab Method (Recap)
Every worklet in this book is built the same way: as a coordinator-plus-workers agentic system that is grown, one capability at a time, through 14 hands-on labs. This chapter is self-contained — it restates the method, the node-graph legend and the pseudocode conventions before applying them to this worklet — so it can be handed to a team on its own.
The guiding design principle is constant across all worklets: agents draft and flag; a named human approves. No worklet takes a consequential action autonomously — the human authority named in this chapter signs off before anything is published or sent.
The four phases
Reading the node graph
Navy — the apniLeap AURA Coordinator: owns the run-state, plan, budgets and audit log.
Light blue — specialist agents, each with one responsibility and a typed message contract.
Amber — the human review gate: a hard stop; the named human is the final authority.
Green — governed publish/act: versioned, auditable, reversible.
Dashed grey — coordinator oversight of every agent; a red back-edge is validation → regenerate.
Pseudocode conventions
Pseudocode is language-neutral: Coordinator orchestrates; Agent.step(ctx) is one specialist doing its job on the shared run-context; a human gate returns approve / edit / override.
The blueprint/plan is locked before work begins; validation is a hard gate; the human approves before publish.
5.1  Worklet Overview
Curriculum revision cycles rely on manually comparing the syllabus against industry job postings and ACM/APNI guidelines — work that takes weeks each time and lags behind how fast industry skills change.
This worklet proposes a Curriculum Intelligence System that maps the current curriculum, aggregates industry-demanded skills, benchmarks against ACM and APNI standards, identifies gaps across all three views, and recommends specific, evidence-linked revisions for HoD/Dean/Board of Studies approval — turning an infrequent, manual review into one auditable, continuously refreshed picture.
Stakeholders: Faculty, HoD, Dean (Academics), Board of Studies
Problems heard
Curriculum revision relies on manually comparing the syllabus against industry job postings and ACM/APNI guidelines, taking weeks each time.
Industry-skill trends change faster than the curriculum review cycle, so gaps go unnoticed until placement or industry feedback surfaces them.
Benchmarking against ACM and APNI standards is done inconsistently across departments.
Recommendations for revision are ad hoc and not tied back to a specific gap or benchmark.
There is no continuously maintained, evidence-backed view of where the curriculum stands.
5.2  Target Final Solution (Definition of Done)
The capstone (Lab 14) is complete when, for a real case, the worklet turns a request into a validated, evidence-grounded curriculum revision recommendation that passes every check, is reviewed and approved by the curriculum committee / HoD, and is published with a full audit trail — with light human edits, not a rewrite.
Capabilities
Success metrics (KPIs)
100% of validation checks pass before the curriculum committee / HoD review (e.g. every recommendation cites its evidence).
Every curriculum revision recommendation is grounded in accreditation criteria and outcome-attainment data, with an evidence record.
Zero consequential actions without the curriculum committee / HoD's approval; a complete audit trail and one-click rollback.
5.3  System at a Glance — the Node Graph
A coordinator-plus-workers system: the apniLeap AURA Coordinator holds the plan, run-state and audit log and drives the pipeline; validation is a hard gate; the human review is a hard stop before publish.

Figure 5 — the Curriculum Intelligence System agent graph. Amber = human gate; green = governed publish; dashed = coordinator oversight.
End-to-end flow
The department links the current curriculum, course outcomes, and credit structure, along with industry-partner and alumni feedback channels.
The Curriculum Mapping, Industry Skills, and ACM/APNI Benchmarking agents each build their respective view in parallel.
The Gap Analysis Agent compares the three views to identify missing topics, outdated content, and skill mismatches.
The Recommendation Agent proposes specific revisions — new courses/electives, updated outcomes, topics to add or retire — tied to each identified gap.
HoD/Dean (Academics)/Board of Studies reviews the gap analysis and recommendations, approves or adjusts them, and approved revisions are released for curriculum adoption.

Lab 1 — Agent vs Chatbot — the curriculum revision recommendation planner loop
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Establish the agent loop (clarify → plan → revise) instead of a one-shot chatbot answer.
What it adds to this worklet.  A minimal planner that clarifies the request, proposes a plan for producing the curriculum revision recommendation, and revises on feedback — the seed this worklet grows from.
Nodes activated.  Coordinator loop (no specialist nodes yet).
Pitfall to avoid.  Don't answer in one shot — without clarify/revise it's a chatbot, not an agent.
Lab 1 — plan & revise

Lab 2 — Tool-Using Agent — call data & compute
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Give the agent tools it must call to act, rather than answering from memory.
What it adds to this worklet.  Readers for syllabi & CO/PO maps and industry & accreditation signals, plus a domain calculator/checker, so the planner acts on real data.
Nodes activated.  Tool layer feeding the early data agents.
Pitfall to avoid.  Never assert a fact in the prompt that a tool should verify.
Lab 2 — typed tools

Lab 3 — Skill Creation — reusable plan & format skills
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Package repeatable capability as reusable, schema-typed skills.
What it adds to this worklet.  A plan/blueprint skill and a formatting skill that renders the curriculum revision recommendation — both reusable across cases.
Nodes activated.  Plan node + Assembly node (as skills).
Pitfall to avoid.  Keep skills case-agnostic and typed; hard-coding one case breaks reuse.
Lab 3 — skill interface

Lab 4 — Memory & Retrieval — state vs stored
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Separate short-term run state from long-term stored knowledge.
What it adds to this worklet.  Session state (the current draft) vs stored reference — syllabi & CO/PO maps and the history of past course / COs — retrieved by tags and by similarity.
Nodes activated.  Retrieval node; seeds validation/no-repeat.
Pitfall to avoid.  Don't conflate run state with the archive — mixing them corrupts the baseline.
Lab 4 — memory split

Lab 5 — MCP-Style Connector — one server for all sources
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Expose every data source through one typed connector.
What it adds to this worklet.  A single connector fronting syllabi & CO/PO maps, industry & accreditation signals and student performance data so every agent reaches data the same governed way.
Nodes activated.  Connector under the data agents.
Pitfall to avoid.  No agent should touch a source directly; a bypass defeats governance later.
Lab 5 — connector

Lab 6 — Runtime — skills, tools, memory, audit
Phase: A · Foundations     Goal: Run the agent inside a controlled runtime with a full audit log.
What it adds to this worklet.  A runtime binding skills + tools + memory behind the loop, enforcing budgets and approval gates and logging every step — the backbone later labs plug into.
Nodes activated.  Coordinator runtime around all nodes.
Pitfall to avoid.  Log inputs and outputs, not just messages; checkpoint before risky steps or resume loses state.
Lab 6 — controlled loop

Lab 7 — Agentic Node Graph — the full pipeline
Phase: B · Orchestration     Goal: Wire the specialist agents into one explicit graph with state and branching.
What it adds to this worklet.  The complete pipeline as a node graph: Curriculum Mapping Agent, Industry Skills Agent, ACM/APNI Benchmarking Agent → … → the curriculum committee / HoD gate → publish, with a validation → regenerate back-edge.
Nodes activated.  All core nodes + the back-edge.
Pitfall to avoid.  Bound the retry edge — an unbounded regenerate loop can spin forever.
Lab 7 — graph

Lab 8 — Parallel Swarm — many course / COs, then merge
Phase: B · Orchestration     Goal: Parallelise the per-unit work and select the best.
What it adds to this worklet.  Several worker agents process course / COs in parallel; a merge/selector picks the best per the plan — cutting latency and improving quality.
Nodes activated.  Parallel worker fan-out + merge.
Pitfall to avoid.  Merge strictly by plan alignment; picking the 'nicest' output quietly breaks coverage.
Lab 8 — fan-out/in

Lab 9 — Deep Research — ground & evidence
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Ground every output in cited, in-scope evidence.
What it adds to this worklet.  For each course / CO the agent builds an evidence record grounded in accreditation criteria and outcome-attainment data, so validity is auditable and out-of-scope content can't slip through.
Nodes activated.  Grounding step into the validation check.
Pitfall to avoid.  Require a real source; a plausible-sounding but ungrounded item must be rejected, not kept.
Lab 9 — grounding

Lab 10 — Ops Resilience — validate, diagnose, fix
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Make the pipeline self-healing when validation fails.
What it adds to this worklet.  When a check fails (every recommendation cites its evidence; CO/PO mapping stays consistent), the agent diagnoses the exact failing constraint and regenerates only the offending part.
Nodes activated.  Validation node + self-healing loop.
Pitfall to avoid.  Regenerate only the failing part — rebuilding everything wastes budget and churns good work.
Lab 10 — diagnose & fix

Lab 11 — SDLC Factory — spec → tested output
Phase: C · Applied reliability     Goal: Treat the plan as a spec and the output as a tested implementation.
What it adds to this worklet.  The worklet becomes a factory: plan in, a curriculum revision recommendation out that provably passes an automated acceptance suite before the curriculum committee / HoD sees it.
Nodes activated.  Validation gates the whole graph.
Pitfall to avoid.  The acceptance suite is the gate; never let a result skip to the human without a green report.
Lab 11 — spec→artifact

Lab 12 — Safety & Governance — approval, eval, rollback
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Add confidentiality, approval, evaluation and rollback.
What it adds to this worklet.  Access control and confidentiality, the the curriculum committee / HoD approval gate as a hard stop, evaluation metrics, versioning and one-click rollback.
Nodes activated.  Human gate + governance node.
Pitfall to avoid.  Confidentiality and rollback are requirements, not extras.
Lab 12 — governed release

Lab 13 — Discipline-Specific Agents — domain specialisation
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Specialise the worklet to its domain variants.
What it adds to this worklet.  Pluggable specialists — program packs (UG/PG, per discipline) — so outputs match the specific style and constraints of each case.
Nodes activated.  Specialist into the generation node.
Pitfall to avoid.  Every specialist output needs a checkable scheme; clever but vague outputs are unusable.
Lab 13 — specialist plug-in

Lab 14 — Capstone — team-owned system
Phase: D · Governance & capstone     Goal: Integrate everything into a deployed, team-owned system.
What it adds to this worklet.  The full worklet: connector + runtime + node graph + parallel work + grounding + self-healing + governance + specialists, with a the curriculum committee / HoD dashboard — delivered and demoed end to end.
Nodes activated.  Every node, deployed.
Pitfall to avoid.  Integrate, don't rewrite — if the labs kept the contracts, the capstone is wiring.
Lab 14 — end-to-end

5.11  Reference Architecture, Pilot & MVP
Reference architecture
Data capture: curriculum documents, job-posting feeds, ACM/APNI standards, industry/alumni feedback.
Data layer: canonical schema for curriculum, skills, benchmarks, gaps, and recommendations.
AI layer: curriculum mapping, skill aggregation, benchmark matching, gap and recommendation analysis.
Workflow layer: Board of Studies review queue, recommendation approval, dashboard.
Governance layer: source citation, department data confidentiality, audit log of adopted revisions.
Pilot artifacts
A curriculum-mapping and industry-skills connector for one department pilot.
An ACM/APNI benchmarking pipeline.
A gap-analysis and recommendation engine.
A curriculum intelligence dashboard with an exportable revision proposal.
Suggested MVP roadmap
Risks & mitigations
5.12  Guardrails, Metrics, Milestones & Glossary
Guardrails
Do not release a curriculum revision recommendation to Board of Studies without evidence tied to a specific gap.
Keep one department's curriculum data confidential from another.
Never adopt a revision without HoD/Dean (Academics)/Board of Studies approval.
Cite the source, date, or clause behind every skill trend and benchmark comparison.
Keep an audit log of recommendations and approvals.
Delivery milestones

Glossary

CHAPTER 6
Question Paper Setting & OBE Analysis System
Blueprint-compliant paper generation with Bloom's/CO-PO tagging, plus full OBE analysis of the assembled paper — before faculty release.
Human authority: the faculty setter & CoE.   Primary output: question paper and OBE analysis.
The 14-Lab Method (Recap)
Every worklet in this book is built the same way: as a coordinator-plus-workers agentic system that is grown, one capability at a time, through 14 hands-on labs. This chapter is self-contained — it restates the method, the node-graph legend and the pseudocode conventions before applying them to this worklet — so it can be handed to a team on its own.
The guiding design principle is constant across all worklets: agents draft and flag; a named human approves. No worklet takes a consequential action autonomously — the human authority named in this chapter signs off before anything is published or sent.
The four phases
Reading the node graph
Navy — the apniLeap AURA Coordinator: owns the run-state, plan, budgets and audit log.
Light blue — specialist agents, each with one responsibility and a typed message contract.
Amber — the human review gate: a hard stop; the named human is the final authority.
Green — governed publish/act: versioned, auditable, reversible.
Dashed grey — coordinator oversight of every agent; a red back-edge is validation → regenerate.
Pseudocode conventions
Pseudocode is language-neutral: Coordinator orchestrates; Agent.step(ctx) is one specialist doing its job on the shared run-context; a human gate returns approve / edit / override.
The blueprint/plan is locked before work begins; validation is a hard gate; the human approves before publish.
6.1  Worklet Overview
Setting a question paper and checking its Outcome-Based Education (OBE) quality are two halves of the same job: both revolve around the approved question bank, the blueprint, Bloom's-level balance, and CO-PO mapping. Today paper setting is manual and error-prone, while OBE analysis (Bloom balance, CO-PO attainment, difficulty, coverage, quality) is often done only after printing, if at all.
This worklet merges both into one system: it assembles a compliant paper from the tagged question bank and blueprint, avoiding recent repeats, then runs a full OBE analysis — Bloom's distribution, CO-PO mapping, difficulty/marks-time balance, blueprint coverage, and quality review — and generates a matching scheme of valuation, all before release to faculty/HoD/COE for approval.
Stakeholders: Faculty, HoD, COE (Exam Cell), IQAC
Problems heard
Faculty spend hours manually picking questions and checking unit-wise/Bloom's distribution by hand.
Blueprints and marks distribution are applied inconsistently across sections of the same course.
Papers sometimes repeat questions from recent semesters because there is no automated repeat check.
CO-PO mapping is done manually and inconsistently, weakening NBA/NAAC attainment data.
Difficulty balance, coverage, and quality issues (ambiguous wording, marks mismatches) are caught only during the exam.
6.2  Target Final Solution (Definition of Done)
The capstone (Lab 14) is complete when, for a real case, the worklet turns a request into a validated, evidence-grounded question paper and OBE analysis that passes every check, is reviewed and approved by the faculty setter & CoE, and is published with a full audit trail — with light human edits, not a rewrite.
Capabilities
Success metrics (KPIs)
100% of validation checks pass before the faculty setter & CoE review (e.g. marks total equals the paper maximum).
Every question paper and OBE analysis is grounded in the syllabus and prescribed texts, with an evidence record.
Zero consequential actions without the faculty setter & CoE's approval; a complete audit trail and one-click rollback.
6.3  System at a Glance — the Node Graph
A coordinator-plus-workers system: the apniLeap AURA Coordinator holds the plan, run-state and audit log and drives the pipeline; validation is a hard gate; the human review is a hard stop before publish.

Figure 6 — the Question Paper Setting & OBE Analysis System agent graph. Amber = human gate; green = governed publish; dashed = coordinator oversight.
End-to-end flow
Faculty/COE loads the approved question bank, the course blueprint (unit weightage, marks pattern, Bloom's targets), the CO list, and the CO-PO articulation matrix.
The Question Bank Agent retrieves candidate questions while the Blueprint Agent loads structural requirements, in parallel.
The Bloom's Taxonomy Agent confirms or tags each candidate question's cognitive level.
The Question Selection Agent assembles a compliant set matching unit coverage, marks, and Bloom's distribution, avoiding recently-used questions.
The CO-PO Mapping Agent tags each selected question to its CO(s) and linked PO(s); the Difficulty & Coverage Agent scores difficulty, marks-time balance, and blueprint coverage.
The Quality & Validation Agent checks wording, duplicates, and blueprint deviations, and generates a matching scheme of valuation.
Faculty/HoD/COE/IQAC reviews the paper, OBE analysis, CO-PO attainment tags, and scheme of valuation, approves or requests changes, and releases it for printing.

6.11  Reference Architecture, Pilot & MVP
Reference architecture
Data capture: approved question bank, course blueprint, CO list, CO-PO articulation matrix.
Data layer: canonical schema for questions, blueprint, tags, assembled paper, scheme, and analysis.
AI layer: Bloom's tagging, blueprint-compliant selection, CO-PO mapping, difficulty/coverage and quality analysis.
Workflow layer: faculty review queue, scheme generation, approval, dashboard.
Governance layer: bank confidentiality, repeat-override control, audit log of released papers and CO-PO data.
Pilot artifacts
A tagged question-bank connector and blueprint/CO/CO-PO intake for one course pilot.
A Bloom's-tagging, blueprint-compliant selection, and repeat-check pipeline.
A CO-PO mapping, difficulty/coverage, and quality-analysis report on the assembled paper.
A paper-setting-and-analysis dashboard with an exportable scheme of valuation and approval report.
Suggested MVP roadmap
Risks & mitigations
6.12  Guardrails, Metrics, Milestones & Glossary
Guardrails
Do not release a generated paper, its OBE analysis, or its scheme of valuation for printing without Faculty/HoD/COE/IQAC approval.
Never select a flagged repeat question without an explicit human override.
Never infer a CO-PO link that is not present in the approved matrix; flag questions that do not map cleanly.
Never certify blueprint coverage with an unresolved gap; flag ambiguous or duplicate questions rather than editing them.
Keep the question bank and draft papers confidential to authorized reviewers, with a full audit log.
Delivery milestones

Glossary

CHAPTER 7
Online Course Examination System Agent
From registration to published results, with integrity and faculty sign-off built in.
Human authority: the faculty & CoE.   Primary output: administered online exam with results.
The 14-Lab Method (Recap)
Every worklet in this book is built the same way: as a coordinator-plus-workers agentic system that is grown, one capability at a time, through 14 hands-on labs. This chapter is self-contained — it restates the method, the node-graph legend and the pseudocode conventions before applying them to this worklet — so it can be handed to a team on its own.
The guiding design principle is constant across all worklets: agents draft and flag; a named human approves. No worklet takes a consequential action autonomously — the human authority named in this chapter signs off before anything is published or sent.
The four phases
Reading the node graph
Navy — the apniLeap AURA Coordinator: owns the run-state, plan, budgets and audit log.
Light blue — specialist agents, each with one responsibility and a typed message contract.
Amber — the human review gate: a hard stop; the named human is the final authority.
Green — governed publish/act: versioned, auditable, reversible.
Dashed grey — coordinator oversight of every agent; a red back-edge is validation → regenerate.
Pseudocode conventions
Pseudocode is language-neutral: Coordinator orchestrates; Agent.step(ctx) is one specialist doing its job on the shared run-context; a human gate returns approve / edit / override.
The blueprint/plan is locked before work begins; validation is a hard gate; the human approves before publish.
7.1  Worklet Overview
Conducting an online course examination involves registration and eligibility checks, secure delivery, integrity monitoring, grading (objective and subjective), grievance handling, and result publishing — each step currently coordinated manually across different tools.
This worklet proposes an Online Course Examination System Agent that manages the full exam lifecycle: registration, delivery, proctoring/integrity checks, automatic grading of objective questions, assisted grading of subjective answers, and result publishing — with faculty approving grades and any integrity flags before results go out.
Stakeholders: Faculty, COE (Exam Cell), Students
Problems heard
Verifying student eligibility (fees, attendance, prerequisite clearance) before registration is manually cross-checked.
Ensuring exam delivery works consistently for a large, distributed student batch is operationally demanding.
Monitoring for integrity issues (multiple logins, unusual timing) is hard to do consistently by hand.
Grading subjective answers for a large batch consistently and quickly is a major time cost.
Post-result revaluation or grievance requests are tracked inconsistently across students.
7.2  Target Final Solution (Definition of Done)
The capstone (Lab 14) is complete when, for a real case, the worklet turns a request into a validated, evidence-grounded administered online exam with results that passes every check, is reviewed and approved by the faculty & CoE, and is published with a full audit trail — with light human edits, not a rewrite.
Capabilities
Success metrics (KPIs)
100% of validation checks pass before the faculty & CoE review (e.g. each candidate gets a unique paper).
Every administered online exam with results is grounded in the approved question pool and rubric, with an evidence record.
Zero consequential actions without the faculty & CoE's approval; a complete audit trail and one-click rollback.
7.3  System at a Glance — the Node Graph
A coordinator-plus-workers system: the apniLeap AURA Coordinator holds the plan, run-state and audit log and drives the pipeline; validation is a hard gate; the human review is a hard stop before publish.

Figure 7 — the Online Course Examination System Agent agent graph. Amber = human gate; green = governed publish; dashed = coordinator oversight.
End-to-end flow
The Registration & Eligibility Agent checks fee status, attendance, and prerequisite clearance before allowing registration.
The Exam Delivery Agent releases the exam within the defined delivery window and manages session access.
The Proctoring & Integrity Agent monitors session activity and flags suspected integrity issues for review.
The Auto-Grading Agent grades objective questions immediately against the answer key.
The Subjective Grading Assist Agent drafts grades for descriptive answers against the rubric.
Faculty reviews integrity flags and subjective grading drafts, approving or adjusting each.
The Grievance & Revaluation Agent tracks any post-result requests through to resolution.
The Result Publishing & Archive Agent publishes approved results and archives the exam record.
